I've been late to my retail job several times and messed up my opening routines, which even got me a gentle warning from my manager. On top of that, my partner and son keep asking what's wrong because I'm completely checked out during dinner and find myself snapping at them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been having any recurring, intrusive memories or thoughts about the freeway accident since it happened, or if you've been trying to avoid thinking or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with both of those things. To avoid those feelings, I've been taking side streets that add twenty minutes to my commute, and I'll shut down or leave the room if anyone tries to talk about the crash.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any nightmares related to the freeway accident, or waking up with memories of it that leave you feeling distressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or woken up with those memories. That hasn't been happening to me, fortunately."

cooking or a coworker making a small mistake with the register makes me snap and raise my voice in a way that surprises even me, and then I feel guilty afterward because it’s not really about them.\n- Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Since I saw that bad freeway accident a couple of weeks ago, I feel like I’m moving through life in a fog. It's like I’m watching myself from the outside, and nothing feels quite real anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling like you're watching yourself from the outside, and how often this feeling occurs since the accident?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I feel like I'm just watching myself go through the motions of talking to customers or making dinner, but I'm slightly outside my body and everything feels muted and far away, like I'm in a movie. This has been happening almost constantly since that night on the freeway two weeks ago. Even when I'm sitting at my pottery wheel, it just doesn't feel real.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant distress or impairment in your social, occupational, or other areas of functioning since this feeling of detachment started, or has it been more of a numb, uncomfortable feeling without much impact on your daily life?\n\nPatient: It has definitely had a huge impact, and I feel like I'm barely holding things together at work or at home. I've been late to my retail job several times and messed up my opening routines, which even got me a gentle warning from my manager. On top of that, my partner and son keep asking what's wrong because I'm completely checked out during dinner and find myself snapping at them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been having any recurring, intrusive memories or thoughts about the freeway accident since it happened, or if you've been trying to avoid thinking or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with both of those things. If I hear tires on wet pavement or see brake lights, I suddenly picture the car flipping and the teenager's bloody face as if I'm right back there, and my chest tightens up. To avoid those feelings, I've been taking side streets that add twenty minutes to my commute, and I'll shut down or leave the room if anyone tries to talk about the crash.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any nightmares related to the freeway accident, or waking up with memories of it that leave you feeling distressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or woken up with those memories. That hasn't been happening to me, fortunately.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall specific details about the accident itself, such as what happened right before or after it occurred, and have you noticed any gaps in your memory since then?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any memory gaps; if anything, I remember everything far too clearly. I was driving home from my pottery circle on a rainy night when I saw the multi-car crash and a car flipping right in front of me. I pulled over to help a dazed teenager out of the wreckage, and his bloody face is an image that keeps flashing back to me constantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience a full range of emotions, such as happiness or sadness, since the accident, or has your emotional response felt somewhat muted or restricted?"

My emotions definitely feel very muted and restricted right now. Because I feel like I'm watching myself from the outside like I'm in a movie, any feelings of happiness or sadness seem really far away and not quite real. It's like I'm too detached from my own body to actually experience them.

To avoid those feelings, I've been taking side streets that add twenty minutes to my commute, and I'll shut down or leave the room if anyone tries to talk about the crash.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any nightmares related to the freeway accident, or waking up with memories of it that leave you feeling distressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or woken up with those memories. That hasn't been happening to me, fortunately.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall specific details about the accident itself, such as what happened right before or after it occurred, and have you noticed any gaps in your memory since then?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any memory gaps; if anything, I remember everything far too clearly. I was driving home from my pottery circle on a rainy night when I saw the multi-car crash and a car flipping right in front of me. I pulled over to help a dazed teenager out of the wreckage, and his bloody face is an image that keeps flashing back to me constantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience a full range of emotions, such as happiness or sadness, since the accident, or has your emotional response felt somewhat muted or restricted?\n\nPatient: My emotions definitely feel very muted and restricted right now. Because I feel like I'm watching myself from the outside like I'm in a movie, any feelings of happiness or sadness seem really far away and not quite real. It's like I'm too detached from my own body to actually experience them.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you couldn't recall how you got somewhere, what you did, or other important aspects of your life since the accident, or has it primarily been this feeling of detachment and watching yourself from the outside?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't had any gaps in my memory or forgotten how I got somewhere. It is really just that constant feeling of detachment, where I'm watching myself from the outside and everything around me feels muted and unreal.

On top of that, my partner and son keep asking what's wrong because I'm completely checked out during dinner and find myself snapping at them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been having any recurring, intrusive memories or thoughts about the freeway accident since it happened, or if you've been trying to avoid thinking or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with both of those things. If I hear tires on wet pavement or see brake lights, I suddenly picture the car flipping and the teenager's bloody face as if I'm right back there, and my chest tightens up. To avoid those feelings, I've been taking side streets that add twenty minutes to my commute, and I'll shut down or leave the room if anyone tries to talk about the crash.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any nightmares related to the freeway accident, or waking up with memories of it that leave you feeling distressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or woken up with those memories. That hasn't been happening to me, fortunately.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall specific details about the accident itself, such as what happened right before or after it occurred, and have you noticed any gaps in your memory since then?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any memory gaps; if anything, I remember everything far too clearly. I was driving home from my pottery circle on a rainy night when I saw the multi-car crash and a car flipping right in front of me. I pulled over to help a dazed teenager out of the wreckage, and his bloody face is an image that keeps flashing back to me constantly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience a full range of emotions, such as happiness or sadness, since the accident, or has your emotional response felt somewhat muted or restricted?\n\nPatient: My emotions definitely feel very muted and restricted right now. Because I feel like I'm watching myself from the outside like I'm in a movie, any feelings of happiness or sadness seem really far away and not quite real. It's like I'm too detached from my own body to actually experience them.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you couldn't recall how you got somewhere, what you did, or other important aspects of your life since the accident, or has it primarily been this feeling of detachment and watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any gaps in my memory or forgotten how I got somewhere. It is really just that constant feeling of detachment, where I'm watching myself from the outside and everything around me feels muted and unreal.\n\nDoctor: How long do these episodes of feeling detached and watching yourself from the outside typically last, and do they seem to be triggered by anything in particular or just happen randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn't really feel like short, isolated episodes, but more like a near-constant state that has been there most of the time since the accident. It isn't sparked by any specific trigger; I just feel slightly outside my body while going through my daily routines like cooking, working, or doing pottery. It's just always there in the background rather than coming and going."
  }
]

On top of that, my partner and son keep asking what's wrong because I'm completely checked out during dinner and find myself snapping at them.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've been having any recurring, intrusive memories or thoughts about the freeway accident since it happened, or if you've been trying to avoid thinking or talking about it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with both of those things. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
